commons)</figcaption></figure></div><p>Two loosely related cybercrime groups operating scores of fake ad agencies from China are so deeply embedded in the ad tech industry that they can launch attacks that surpass the scale of the largest advertisers. At a time when China is under intense international scrutiny, these groups largely fly under the radar, raking in millions of victims in Europe and the US without drawing attention to their source.</p><p><span>In this article, we present </span><strong>eGobbler</strong><span> and their doppelganger </span><strong>Nephos7</strong><span>, their origins and what made them successful over time.</span></p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Setting the stage — Rise to success, 2017–2018</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" class="icon-noB79L"><g><path d="M2.53001 7.81595C3.49179 4.73911 6.43281 2.5 9.91173 2.5C13.1684 2.5 15.9537 4.46214 stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-maximize2 lucide-maximize-2 icon-noB79L"><polyline points="15 3 21 3 21 9"></polyline><polyline points="9 21 3 21 3 15"></polyline><line x1="21" x2="14" y1="3" y2="10"></line><line x1="3" x2="10" y1="21" y2="14"></line></svg></button></div></div></div></a><figcaption class="image-caption">The infamous Amazon gift card scam (eGobbler, iOS, July 2018)</figcaption></figure></div><p><strong>eGobbler</strong><span> rose to success by being one of the first groups to leverage javascript fingerprinting to target iOS. iOS being the least fragmented mobile environment, they understood that they could build very precise fingerprints for it, that would allow them to evade simulated environments as found in the security scanners of that time. Starting with </span><a href="https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Sensor_APIs">JavaScript Sensor APIs</a><span> and quickly moving to WebGL based fingerprinting, they essentially defeated user agent spoofing and gained unfettered Javascript execution on millions of end-users’ devices since then.</span></p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Stable scheme, with some twists</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§stable-scheme-with-some-twists"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/stable-scheme-with-some-twists"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>Since 2018, eGobbler has settled on a relatively sophisticated but slow evolving stack:</p><ul><li><p><span>Leverage ad platforms’ hosting to embed malicious code in HTML5 ad code. This is typically achieved by injecting the code in </span><a href="https://createjs.com/">CreateJS</a><span> or </span><a href="https://greensock.com/">Greensock</a><span> dependencies — libraries commonly found in HTML5 ads.</span></p></li><li><p>Use of WebGL-based fingerprinting (among other tricks) to activate on victims’ devices only</p></li><li><p>Geo-fencing, using basic server-side cloaking</p></li><li><p>One-time use of commercial CDNs as reverse proxies (namely Rackspace and Fastly)</p></li><li><p>Programmatic ad chains are typically made of an SSP call, a DSP call and optionally an adserver call. eGobbler is adept at creating artifical chains of ad tag redirections between multiple DSPs, which would never exist in the wild. The goal is likely to confuse analysts on the actual source of the ad.</p></li><li><p>Through 2019, eGobbler slowly migrated to weekend activity. By 2020, they ended up exclusively running on weekends (and holidays) to maximize impact during off hours.</p></li></ul><blockquote><p>Considering eGobbler’s massive scale, this change of tactic alone is the main driver for the rise in general malvertising weekend activity since 2019</p></blockquote><p><strong>Breaking the browsers</strong></p><p><span>Top malvertising groups see increased browser security around forced redirects as a threat and invest in finding flaws. To boost the efficacy of their payloads, eGobbler came up with a number of </span><strong>zero day exploits</strong><span>.</span></p><p><span>Detected by Confiant in 2019, CVE-2019–8771 and CVE-2019–5840 are browser vulnerabilities (Safari and Chrome) introduced by eGobbler and allowing them to bypass popup blocking and iframe sandboxing, which are protections against forced redirects (more </span><a href="https://blog.confiant.com/massive-egobbler-malvertising-campaign-leverages-chrome-vulnerability-to-target-ios-users-a534b95a037f">here</a><span> and </span><a href="https://blog.confiant.com/malvertiser-egobbler-exploits-chrome-webkit-bugs-infects-over-1-billion-ads-6b8ccc41b0e6">here</a><span>).</span></p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Enter Nephos7</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§enter-nephos7"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/enter-nephos7"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>Nephos7 started out in Q4 of 2019 with familiar tactics and techniques:</p><ul><li><p>Use of commercial ad servers to hide malicious code</p></li><li><p>Use of commercial CDNs as reverse proxies (Cloudfront)</p></li><li><p>Mimick instantiation of popular JavaScript APIs (e.g. Hotjar, Snowplow, etc.) to hide in plain sight</p></li><li><p>Use of WebGL-based fingerprinting (among other tricks) to activate on victims’ devices only</p></li><li><p>By end-of-year 2019, Nephos7 had aligned with eGobbler’s weekend patterns with a large attack on December 29.</p></li></ul><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><a class="image-link image2 graphics card vendors (April 2020) — comments added for clarity</p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Timeline of tactics and payloads</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex the landing pages bear a striking resemblance. As an illustration, below is a comparison of carrier-branded “CC-Submit” scams where the victim is presented with a fake message from their ISP (or mobile phone carrier) inviting them to enter their credit card information to confirm their prize.</p><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><div class="image-link image2 can-restack" data-component-name="Image2ToDOM"><div class="image2-inset"><picture><source type="image/webp" y1="3" y2="10"></line><line x1="3" x2="10" y1="21" y2="14"></line></svg></button></div></div></div></a></figure></div><p>Fake Flash “Holcus” Drive-by Download — Left side: eGobbler, July 25, USA | Right side: Nephos7, July 26, USA</p><p><strong><span>Introducing “Holcus Installer”, the adware dropped by Nephos7 and eGobbler, research by </span><a href="https://twitter.com/lordx64/">Taha Karim</a><span>- Lead Security Researcher, Confiant</span></strong></p><blockquote><p><em>eGobbler July25 campaign downloads a .msi file with the following sha-256 hash c818fe4c3fd3b0dbcfc3f17440e110c5a6ce3729382ffc88db8f83f830a115f9</em></p><p><em>Nephos7 July 26 campaign downloads a .msi file with the following sha-256 hash fb7d3f3914bf1722b3b369b23509b3746a44496bd3c78de91f27f8ee8d0ebead</em></p></blockquote><p><span>Both of these .msi files unpack and run the same variant of a signed installer we dubbed </span><em>Holcus Installer</em><span>. The Holcus Installer samples we collected so far were all signed, and some of their features include:</span></p><ul><li><p><span>C2 communications via https, with certificate pinning checks. For example checks are performed on the certificate issuer string if it contains a substring </span><em>CloudFlare</em><span>, </span><em>Cloudflare</em><span>, or </span><em>Let’s Encrypt</em><span> before a communication is established.</span></p></li><li><p><span>All Holcus Installer strings hinting to main functionality, C2 server, and executed commands are all encrypted with a custom RC4-like algorithm using key </span><em>2KNl6BKD94L2LyAY</em></p></li><li><p><span>Holcus Installer checks if PUA detection (PuaProtection field) is enabled in the current Windows Defender configuration. This is done by via the wmi query: </span><em>SELECT * FROM MSFT_MpPreference</em></p></li><li><p><span>One variant of Holcus Installer we found, additionally checks the DeviceID of the keyboard retreived via wmi query: </span><em>SELECT * FROM Win32_Keyboard</em></p></li><li><p>Holcus Installer has the capability to download and execute binaries from the C2 server and sends hardcoded status messages to the C2 server by encoding them using a bin2hex encoding.</p></li><li><p><span>Holcus Installer variants we collected have been observed communicating with the following C&amp;C servers: </span><em>myappcube[.]com, businessfulcrumsoft[.]com and softwaredatariver[.]com</em></p></li><li><p><span>Holcus Installer uses a hardcoded User-Agent “</span><em>NSIS_Inetc (Mozilla)</em><span>” for C2 communications</span></p></li><li><p>In our test environment, Holcus Installer was seen downloading and installing a decoy copy of notepad++ (sha256 hash bda85bc0bb7beb11dbb9e9a964a2e2f0e4d35d0fc1e6b769e32b6847bfed8296)</p></li></ul><h3 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Explaining the striking resemblance</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§explaining-the-striking-resemblance"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/explaining-the-striking-resemblance"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h3><p>We think that the two groups are solely focused on redirecting visitors to malicious “offers” that are operated by different groups, themselves specialized in operating these schemes. Malvertisers can shop around with different affiliate marketers to get the best yield for their traffic. Somehow Nephos7 and eGobbler are shopping around at the same stores.</p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>2020’s Largest Digital Marketers</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex achieved “web scale” on multiple occasions. No other malvertising group was (ever?) able to create anything comparable to this massive ecosystem disruption. Confiant)</figcaption></figure></div><blockquote><p><strong>Clocking at 5% of all display advertising on May 24th, Nephos7 arguably became (for an instant) the largest digital marketer in Europe. In </strong><span>comparison</span><strong>, eGobbler’s most significant spike “only” hit 2.3% on July 25th.</strong></p></blockquote><p>In total, in 2020, Confiant blocked 112 million ads from eGobbler and 198 million ads from Nephos7 on behalf of our online publisher clients. Extrapolating our data, we estimate 6 billion malicious ads were served by the two actors during the period.</p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Targeting</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§targeting"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/targeting"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>Device/OS targeting has been markedly different between the two actors. In 2020, eGobbler was heavily focused on targeting desktop computers (76%) while Nephos7 was more evenly split between mobile devices (52%) and desktop (48%).</p><p>As presented earlier, Nephos7 specifically targets Windows, excluding Mac OS X from its targeting.</p><p>Notably, in 2020 iOS only received a small fraction of hits from eGobbler (1.8%) while being completely excluded by Nephos7. Both actors have favored Android as their mobile platform of choice. This is all the more striking as eGobbler started out back in 2017 with a strong iOS focus.</p><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><div class="image-link image2 can-restack" 1456w" sizes="100vw" loading="lazy"/></picture><div></div></div></div></figure></div><p>Device/OS targeting by actor (2020)</p><p>To achieve the sort of persistent scale that both actors enjoyed in 2020, they became experts at building relationships with “DSP” ad platforms. To enter the ad tech ecosystem, they worked to look reputable from all perspectives. That includes:</p><ul><li><p>Building a reputable looking corporate identity, with a legal entity registered far from “home” - more on this below.</p></li><li><p>Using a commercial ad server to host ad creatives and give a semblance of buyer sophistication. In reality, well-tuned commercial ad servers can achieve incredibly powerful cloaking for malvertisers.</p></li><li><p>Running dummy ad campaigns for weeks or months at low volumes (to build reputation) before flipping the switch.</p></li></ul><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Hunting Corporations</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§hunting-corporations"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/hunting-corporations"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>Programmatic advertising has been architectured such that online publishers have little oversight or visibility on campaigns that are running on their sites. What will start running at full throttle at 8 am on a quiet Sunday morning (eGobbler’s and Nephos7’s favorite modus operandi) is left to sheer unpredictability.</p><p>The situation is quite different if you take the perspective of a DSP. To qualify for the kind of scale that these threat actors are craving for, customers typically go through a thorough approval process, mostly focused on assessing risk based on reputation.</p><h3 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Burn and Repeat</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§burn-and-repeat"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/burn-and-repeat"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h3><p>Once they’ve committed their deed, the abused DSP forever bans the offending entity used to finance the malicious ad campaign. This constraint shapes for our attackers a fairly simple game plan:</p><ul><li><p>Create many legal entities,</p></li><li><p>Burn them one by one with each DSP</p></li><li><p>Repeat.</p></li></ul><p><span>Due to the lack of industry-wide buyer transparency, malicious entities have the leisure to strike repeatedly without fear of industry-wide ban. </span><strong><span>We at Confiant sponsor an initiative to provide this transparency: </span><a href="http://buyersdotjson.com/">buyers.json</a></strong><span>.</span></p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Network of Organized Crime</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§network-of-organized-crime"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/network-of-organized-crime"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" stroke-width="2" stroke-linecap="round" stroke-linejoin="round" class="lucide lucide-link"><path d="M10 13a5 5 0 0 0 7.54.54l3-3a5 5 0 0 0-7.07-7.07l-1.72 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>Over time, Confiant’s security team started to methodically pin attacks to their corresponding legal entities. This effort could not have been possible without the cooperation of many impacted DSPs (thank you!).</p><h3 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Nephos7 Entities</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor 1.71"></path><path d="M14 11a5 5 0 0 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h3><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><a class="image-link image2 y1="3" y2="10"></line><line x1="3" x2="10" y1="21" y2="14"></line></svg></button></div></div></div></a></figure></div><p>Nephos7 entities active in 2020</p><p><span>We believe </span><strong>Wooden Ads</strong><span> started operating some time in Q4 of 2019 as the first Nephos7 front company making the rounds through the major DSPs. With an incorporation in November 2017, one can only wonder what this company was previously used for. The level of sophistication we’ve identified right from its inception is consistent with a previous life in malvertising.</span></p><p><span>Despite a remarkable streak of </span><strong>Wooden Ads</strong><span> activity, it soon became time to invoke more legal entities to establish more DSP connections. By June, Nephos7 started rolling out a wide range of entities that had been patiently staged since 2018. Incorporated in Colorado in March and October 2018 respectively, </span><strong>AdSige</strong><span> and </span><strong>SignalAds </strong><span>both appeared on our radar in June 2020 in separate attacks. In total, between June and November, 8 entities were responsible for Nephos7 attacks. Paradoxically, this is also a time of decreased success, characterized by an inability to generate any significant volumes after July.</span></p><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><a 1456w" sizes="100vw" loading="lazy"/></picture><div></div></div></div></figure></div><p>eGobbler entities active in 2019 and 2020</p><p>Confiant has been tracking eGobbler since 2017 but only started building consistent entity attribution in 2020. We know however that eGobbler started out by registering legal entities in Hong Kong and over time realized that registering in the US would carry more reputation and facilitate building business relationships with DSPs. Two entities were created in August 2019 and another two in March 2020, all with Chinese-named directors.</p><div class="captioned-image-container"><figure><a class="image-link image2 is-viewable-img Biz</strong><span> and </span><strong>InkMediaWorks </strong><span>via LinkedIn</span></p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>Wrapping up</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft pc-display-contents pc-reset pubTheme-yiXxQA"><div class="pencraft pc-reset header-anchor offset-top" id="§wrapping-up"></div><button class="pencraft pc-reset pencraft iconButton-mq_Et5 iconButtonBase-dJGHgN buttonBase-GK1x3M buttonStyle-r7yGCK size_sm-G3LciD priority_secondary-S63h9o" tabindex="0" type="button" data-href="https://blog.confiant.com/i/193830746/wrapping-up"><svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" width="18" height="18" viewBox="0 0 24 24" fill="none" stroke="currentColor" 0-7.54-.54l-3 3a5 5 0 0 0 7.07 7.07l1.71-1.71"></path></svg></button></div></div></h2><p>We believe we’ve achieved a significant level of visibility in both eGobbler’s and Nephos7’s infrastructure. By disclosing our findings, we are hoping to ⑴ wipe out a good amount of their infrastructure in a single blow and ⑵ educate on these threats and how to defend against them.</p><p>One burning question remains: Are Nephos7 and eGobbler two divisions of the same group? Are they competitors? Having collected hundreds of IOCs on both actors, we can confidently say that both infrastructures are completely separated and do not overlap. However, the modes of operation and evolution of tactics are strikingly aligned in unique ways, suggesting that the two Chinese groups are probably aware of each other and tracking each other’s iterations.</p><h2 class="header-anchor-post"><strong>IOCs</strong><div class="pencraft pc-display-flex pc-alignItems-center pc-position-absolute pc-reset header-anchor-parent"><div class="pencraft commons)\u003c/figcaption>\u003c/figure>\u003c/div>\u003cp>Two loosely related cybercrime groups operating scores of fake ad agencies from China are so deeply embedded in the ad tech industry that they can launch attacks that surpass the scale of the largest advertisers. At a time when China is under intense international scrutiny, these groups largely fly under the radar, raking in millions of victims in Europe and the US without drawing attention to their source.\u003c/p>\u003cp>In this article, we present \u003cstrong>eGobbler\u003c/strong> and their doppelganger \u003cstrong>Nephos7\u003c/strong>, their origins and what made them successful over time.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Setting the stage — Rise to success, 2017–2018\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cdiv class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\">\u003cfigure>\u003ca class=\\\"image-link 1456w\\\" sizes=\\\"100vw\\\">\u003c/picture>\u003cdiv class=\\\"image-link-expand\\\">\u003cdiv class=\\\"pencraft pc-display-flex pc-gap-8 pc-reset\\\">\u003cbutton tabindex=\\\"0\\\" 20\\\" fill=\\\"none\\\" stroke-width=\\\"1.5\\\" stroke=\\\"var(--color-fg-primary)\\\" stroke-linecap=\\\"round\\\" stroke-linejoin=\\\"round\\\" xmlns=\\\"http://www.w3.org/2000/svg\\\" class=\\\"icon-noB79L\\\">\u003cg>\u003cpath d=\\\"M2.53001 7.81595C3.49179 4.73911 6.43281 2.5 9.91173 2.5C13.1684 2.5 15.9537 4.46214 17.0852 7.23684L17.6179 8.67647M17.6179 8.67647L18.5002 buttonBase-GK1x3M\\\">\u003csvg xmlns=\\\"http://www.w3.org/2000/svg\\\" width=\\\"20\\\" height=\\\"20\\\" viewBox=\\\"0 0 24 24\\\" fill=\\\"none\\\" stroke=\\\"currentColor\\\" stroke-width=\\\"2\\\" stroke-linecap=\\\"round\\\" stroke-linejoin=\\\"round\\\" class=\\\"lucide lucide-maximize2 lucide-maximize-2 icon-noB79L\\\">\u003cpolyline points=\\\"15 3 21 3 21 9\\\">\u003c/polyline>\u003cpolyline points=\\\"9 21 3 21 3 15\\\">\u003c/polyline>\u003cline x1=\\\"21\\\" x2=\\\"14\\\" y1=\\\"3\\\" y2=\\\"10\\\">\u003c/line>\u003cline x1=\\\"3\\\" x2=\\\"10\\\" y1=\\\"21\\\" y2=\\\"14\\\">\u003c/line>\u003c/svg>\u003c/button>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/a>\u003cfigcaption class=\\\"image-caption\\\">The infamous Amazon gift card scam (eGobbler, iOS, July 2018)\u003c/figcaption>\u003c/figure>\u003c/div>\u003cp>\u003cstrong>eGobbler\u003c/strong> rose to success by being one of the first groups to leverage javascript fingerprinting to target iOS. iOS being the least fragmented mobile environment, they understood that they could build very precise fingerprints for it, that would allow them to evade simulated environments as found in the security scanners of that time. Starting with \u003ca href=\\\"https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/API/Sensor_APIs\\\">JavaScript Sensor APIs\u003c/a> and quickly moving to WebGL based fingerprinting, they essentially defeated user agent spoofing and gained unfettered Javascript execution on millions of end-users’ devices since then.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Stable scheme, with some twists\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>Since 2018, eGobbler has settled on a relatively sophisticated but slow evolving stack:\u003c/p>\u003cul>\u003cli>\u003cp>Leverage ad platforms’ hosting to embed malicious code in HTML5 ad code. This is typically achieved by injecting the code in \u003ca href=\\\"https://createjs.com/\\\">CreateJS\u003c/a> or \u003ca href=\\\"https://greensock.com/\\\">Greensock\u003c/a> dependencies — libraries commonly found in HTML5 ads.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Use of WebGL-based fingerprinting (among other tricks) to activate on victims’ devices only\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Geo-fencing, using basic server-side cloaking\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>One-time use of commercial CDNs as reverse proxies (namely Rackspace and Fastly)\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Programmatic ad chains are typically made of an SSP call, a DSP call and optionally an adserver call. eGobbler is adept at creating artifical chains of ad tag redirections between multiple DSPs, which would never exist in the wild. The goal is likely to confuse analysts on the actual source of the ad.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Through 2019, eGobbler slowly migrated to weekend activity. By 2020, they ended up exclusively running on weekends (and holidays) to maximize impact during off hours.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003c/ul>\u003cblockquote>\u003cp>Considering eGobbler’s massive scale, this change of tactic alone is the main driver for the rise in general malvertising weekend activity since 2019\u003c/p>\u003c/blockquote>\u003cp>\u003cstrong>Breaking the browsers\u003c/strong>\u003c/p>\u003cp>Top malvertising groups see increased browser security around forced redirects as a threat and invest in finding flaws. To boost the efficacy of their payloads, eGobbler came up with a number of \u003cstrong>zero day exploits\u003c/strong>.\u003c/p>\u003cp>Detected by Confiant in 2019, CVE-2019–8771 and CVE-2019–5840 are browser vulnerabilities (Safari and Chrome) introduced by eGobbler and allowing them to bypass popup blocking and iframe sandboxing, which are protections against forced redirects (more \u003ca href=\\\"https://blog.confiant.com/massive-egobbler-malvertising-campaign-leverages-chrome-vulnerability-to-target-ios-users-a534b95a037f\\\">here\u003c/a> and \u003ca href=\\\"https://blog.confiant.com/malvertiser-egobbler-exploits-chrome-webkit-bugs-infects-over-1-billion-ads-6b8ccc41b0e6\\\">here\u003c/a>).\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Enter Nephos7\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>Nephos7 started out in Q4 of 2019 with familiar tactics and techniques:\u003c/p>\u003cul>\u003cli>\u003cp>Use of commercial ad servers to hide malicious code\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Use of commercial CDNs as reverse proxies (Cloudfront)\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Mimick instantiation of popular JavaScript APIs (e.g. Hotjar, Snowplow, etc.) to hide in plain sight\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Use of WebGL-based fingerprinting (among other tricks) to activate on victims’ devices only\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>By end-of-year 2019, Nephos7 had aligned with eGobbler’s weekend patterns with a large attack on December 29.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003c/ul>\u003cdiv Download — Left side: eGobbler, July 25, USA | Right side: Nephos7, July 26, USA\u003c/p>\u003cp>\u003cstrong>Introducing “Holcus Installer”, the adware dropped by Nephos7 and eGobbler, research by \u003ca href=\\\"https://twitter.com/lordx64/\\\">Taha Karim\u003c/a>- Lead Security Researcher, Confiant\u003c/strong>\u003c/p>\u003cblockquote>\u003cp>\u003cem>eGobbler July25 campaign downloads a .msi file with the following sha-256 hash c818fe4c3fd3b0dbcfc3f17440e110c5a6ce3729382ffc88db8f83f830a115f9\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003cp>\u003cem>Nephos7 July 26 campaign downloads a .msi file with the following sha-256 hash fb7d3f3914bf1722b3b369b23509b3746a44496bd3c78de91f27f8ee8d0ebead\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003c/blockquote>\u003cp>Both of these .msi files unpack and run the same variant of a signed installer we dubbed \u003cem>Holcus Installer\u003c/em>. The Holcus Installer samples we collected so far were all signed, and some of their features include:\u003c/p>\u003cul>\u003cli>\u003cp>C2 communications via https, with certificate pinning checks. For example checks are performed on the certificate issuer string if it contains a substring \u003cem>CloudFlare\u003c/em>, \u003cem>Cloudflare\u003c/em>, or \u003cem>Let’s Encrypt\u003c/em> before a communication is established.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>All Holcus Installer strings hinting to main functionality, C2 server, and executed commands are all encrypted with a custom RC4-like algorithm using key \u003cem>2KNl6BKD94L2LyAY\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Holcus Installer checks if PUA detection (PuaProtection field) is enabled in the current Windows Defender configuration. This is done by via the wmi query: \u003cem>SELECT * FROM MSFT_MpPreference\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>One variant of Holcus Installer we found, additionally checks the DeviceID of the keyboard retreived via wmi query: \u003cem>SELECT * FROM Win32_Keyboard\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Holcus Installer has the capability to download and execute binaries from the C2 server and sends hardcoded status messages to the C2 server by encoding them using a bin2hex encoding.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Holcus Installer variants we collected have been observed communicating with the following C&amp;C servers: \u003cem>myappcube[.]com, businessfulcrumsoft[.]com and softwaredatariver[.]com\u003c/em>\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Holcus Installer uses a hardcoded User-Agent “\u003cem>NSIS_Inetc (Mozilla)\u003c/em>” for C2 communications\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>In our test environment, Holcus Installer was seen downloading and installing a decoy copy of notepad++ (sha256 hash bda85bc0bb7beb11dbb9e9a964a2e2f0e4d35d0fc1e6b769e32b6847bfed8296)\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003c/ul>\u003ch3>\u003cstrong>Explaining the striking resemblance\u003c/strong>\u003c/h3>\u003cp>We think that the two groups are solely focused on redirecting visitors to malicious “offers” that are operated by different groups, themselves specialized in operating these schemes. Malvertisers can shop around with different affiliate marketers to get the best yield for their traffic. Somehow Nephos7 and eGobbler are shopping around at the same stores.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>2020’s Largest Digital Marketers\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cdiv display advertising (source: Confiant)\u003c/figcaption>\u003c/figure>\u003c/div>\u003cblockquote>\u003cp>\u003cstrong>Clocking at 5% of all display advertising on May 24th, Nephos7 arguably became (for an instant) the largest digital marketer in Europe. In \u003c/strong>comparison\u003cstrong>, eGobbler’s most significant spike “only” hit 2.3% on July 25th.\u003c/strong>\u003c/p>\u003c/blockquote>\u003cp>In total, in 2020, Confiant blocked 112 million ads from eGobbler and 198 million ads from Nephos7 on behalf of our online publisher clients. Extrapolating our data, we estimate 6 billion malicious ads were served by the two actors during the period.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Targeting\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>Device/OS targeting has been markedly different between the two actors. In 2020, eGobbler was heavily focused on targeting desktop computers (76%) while Nephos7 was more evenly split between mobile devices (52%) and desktop (48%).\u003c/p>\u003cp>As presented earlier, Nephos7 specifically targets Windows, excluding Mac OS X from its targeting.\u003c/p>\u003cp>Notably, in 2020 iOS only received a small fraction of hits from eGobbler (1.8%) while being completely excluded by Nephos7. Both actors have favored Android as their mobile platform of choice. This is all the more striking as eGobbler started out back in 2017 with a strong iOS focus.\u003c/p>\u003cdiv class=\\\"captioned-image-container\\\">\u003cfigure>\u003cdiv class=\\\"image-link 848w, https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!JGIV!,w_1272,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F50623a04-ee64-49a9-aeb2-c0b44369e5a6_700x224.png 1272w, https://substackcdn.com/image/fetch/$s_!JGIV!,w_1456,c_limit,f_auto,q_auto:good,fl_progressive:steep/https%3A%2F%2Fsubstack-post-media.s3.amazonaws.com%2Fpublic%2Fimages%2F50623a04-ee64-49a9-aeb2-c0b44369e5a6_700x224.png 1456w\\\" sizes=\\\"100vw\\\" loading=\\\"lazy\\\">\u003c/picture>\u003cdiv>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/figure>\u003c/div>\u003cp>Device/OS targeting by actor (2020)\u003c/p>\u003cp>To achieve the sort of persistent scale that both actors enjoyed in 2020, they became experts at building relationships with “DSP” ad platforms. To enter the ad tech ecosystem, they worked to look reputable from all perspectives. That includes:\u003c/p>\u003cul>\u003cli>\u003cp>Building a reputable looking corporate identity, with a legal entity registered far from “home” - more on this below.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Using a commercial ad server to host ad creatives and give a semblance of buyer sophistication. In reality, well-tuned commercial ad servers can achieve incredibly powerful cloaking for malvertisers.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Running dummy ad campaigns for weeks or months at low volumes (to build reputation) before flipping the switch.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003c/ul>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Hunting Corporations\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>Programmatic advertising has been architectured such that online publishers have little oversight or visibility on campaigns that are running on their sites. What will start running at full throttle at 8 am on a quiet Sunday morning (eGobbler’s and Nephos7’s favorite modus operandi) is left to sheer unpredictability.\u003c/p>\u003cp>The situation is quite different if you take the perspective of a DSP. To qualify for the kind of scale that these threat actors are craving for, customers typically go through a thorough approval process, mostly focused on assessing risk based on reputation.\u003c/p>\u003ch3>\u003cstrong>Burn and Repeat\u003c/strong>\u003c/h3>\u003cp>Once they’ve committed their deed, the abused DSP forever bans the offending entity used to finance the malicious ad campaign. This constraint shapes for our attackers a fairly simple game plan:\u003c/p>\u003cul>\u003cli>\u003cp>Create many legal entities,\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Burn them one by one with each DSP\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003cli>\u003cp>Repeat.\u003c/p>\u003c/li>\u003c/ul>\u003cp>Due to the lack of industry-wide buyer transparency, malicious entities have the leisure to strike repeatedly without fear of industry-wide ban. \u003cstrong>We at Confiant sponsor an initiative to provide this transparency: \u003ca href=\\\"http://buyersdotjson.com/\\\">buyers.json\u003c/a>\u003c/strong>.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Network of Organized Crime\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>Over time, Confiant’s security team started to methodically pin attacks to their corresponding legal entities. This effort could not have been possible without the cooperation of many impacted DSPs (thank you!).\u003c/p>\u003ch3>\u003cstrong>Nephos7 Entities\u003c/strong>\u003c/h3>\u003cdiv x1=\\\"3\\\" x2=\\\"10\\\" y1=\\\"21\\\" y2=\\\"14\\\">\u003c/line>\u003c/svg>\u003c/button>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/div>\u003c/a>\u003c/figure>\u003c/div>\u003cp>Nephos7 entities active in 2020\u003c/p>\u003cp>We believe \u003cstrong>Wooden Ads\u003c/strong> started operating some time in Q4 of 2019 as the first Nephos7 front company making the rounds through the major DSPs. With an incorporation in November 2017, one can only wonder what this company was previously used for. The level of sophistication we’ve identified right from its inception is consistent with a previous life in malvertising.\u003c/p>\u003cp>Despite a remarkable streak of \u003cstrong>Wooden Ads\u003c/strong> activity, it soon became time to invoke more legal entities to establish more DSP connections. By June, Nephos7 started rolling out a wide range of entities that had been patiently staged since 2018. Incorporated in Colorado in March and October 2018 respectively, \u003cstrong>AdSige\u003c/strong> and \u003cstrong>SignalAds \u003c/strong>both appeared on our radar in June 2020 in separate attacks. In total, between June and November, 8 entities were responsible for Nephos7 attacks. Paradoxically, this is also a time of decreased success, characterized by an inability to generate any significant volumes after July.\u003c/p>\u003cdiv 2020\u003c/p>\u003cp>Confiant has been tracking eGobbler since 2017 but only started building consistent entity attribution in 2020. business persona to \u003cstrong>Media Biz\u003c/strong> and \u003cstrong>InkMediaWorks \u003c/strong>via LinkedIn\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>Wrapping up\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>We believe we’ve achieved a significant level of visibility in both eGobbler’s and Nephos7’s infrastructure. By disclosing our findings, we are hoping to ⑴ wipe out a good amount of their infrastructure in a single blow and ⑵ educate on these threats and how to defend against them.\u003c/p>\u003cp>One burning question remains: Are Nephos7 and eGobbler two divisions of the same group? Are they competitors? Having collected hundreds of IOCs on both actors, we can confidently say that both infrastructures are completely separated and do not overlap. However, the modes of operation and evolution of tactics are strikingly aligned in unique ways, suggesting that the two Chinese groups are probably aware of each other and tracking each other’s iterations.\u003c/p>\u003ch2>\u003cstrong>IOCs\u003c/strong>\u003c/h2>\u003cp>\u003ca href=\\\"https://github.com/WeAreConfiant/security/blob/master/stix-feeds/egobbler-nephos7.json\\\">⭳
